---

**"151 Trading Strategies" (Kakushadze & Serur, 2018)** — 361-page academic compendium covering:

- **Chapter 2**: ~55 options strategies (spreads, butterflies, straddles, condors)
- **Chapter 3**: ~20 equity strategies (momentum, mean-reversion, MA crossovers, pairs trading, KNN ML)
- **Chapter 4**: 6 ETF strategies (sector rotation, dual momentum, IBS mean-reversion, leveraged ETF decay)
- **Chapters 5–19**: Fixed income, FX, volatility, crypto, global macro, etc.
- **Appendix A**: Complete R source code for intraday backtesting with dollar-neutral optimization

---

**What I'm adding to your backtest setup right now:**

A new `backtesting/daily/` framework (alongside the existing ORB engine) with these strategies from the paper:

| H# | Strategy | From paper |
|----|----------|------------|
| H005 | **Dual-Momentum Sector Rotation** — relative (top sectors) + absolute (SPY vs MA200) momentum; hold TLT when market is in downtrend | §4.1.2 |
| H006 | **Sector Rotation + MA Filter** — same but per-sector MA filter instead of market-level | §4.1.1 |
| H007 | **Dual MA Crossover on SPY** — SMA(10)/SMA(30) trend following | §3.12 |
| H008 | **IBS Mean-Reversion** — cross-sectional ETF reversion via Internal Bar Strength | §4.4 |

All tested with regime tags (SPY 200-day SMA), after-tax metrics (STCG/LTCG), 10 bps slippage. Building now — will have results shortly.

<result>Everything runs cleanly. Here is a summary of what was built and the key findings.

---

## What was built

All 8 files created under `/workspace/agent/backtesting/daily/`:

- **`data.py`** — fetches daily OHLCV via Alpaca (primary) or yfinance (fallback), caches as parquet at `backtesting/cache/&lt;hash&gt;_daily_&lt;start&gt;_&lt;end&gt;.parquet`. Alpaca isn't installed in this environment so yfinance is used automatically.

- **`metrics.py`** — `compute_metrics(equity_curve, trades)` returning annualized return, after-tax return (37% STCG if avg hold &lt; 252 days, 20% LTCG otherwise), Sharpe, max drawdown, Calmar, win rate, avg hold days.

- **`signals/ma_crossover.py`** — `single_ma`, `dual_ma`, `triple_ma`.

- **`signals/sector_rotation.py`** — `sector_momentum` (12-1 month, top-N equal weight, executes on last trading day of month), `dual_momentum` (same but routes to TLT when SPY &lt; SMA(200)), `signals_to_weights` helper that forward-fills weights with proper execution-next-day lag.

- **`engine.py`** — `DailyBacktester`: vectorized, processes day-by-day, signals from day t execute at day t+1 close (±slippage). Handles entries, exits, partial rebalances, and end-of-period close-out. Trades log has `date_in`, `date_out`, `symbol`, `pnl`, `hold_days`.

- **`run_h005.py`** — runs all four strategies, tags regimes via SPY SMA(200), prints full metrics + regime breakdown + summary table for both in-sample and OOS.

---

|---|---|---|---|---|---|

**George**: Done. Here's what was built and what the first results show:

---

**Paper ingested** — "151 Trading Strategies" (Kakushadze & Serur, 2018). Strategy catalog filed in the wiki with 151 strategies organized by asset class and implementation priority.

**New daily strategy framework** built at `backtesting/daily/`:
- Vectorized EOD engine with after-tax metrics, slippage, regime tags
- Sector rotation signals (§4.1, §4.1.2) and MA crossover (§3.12)
- Runs in seconds via yfinance data cache

**H005 results — summary:**

| Strategy | After-Tax (IS) | MaxDD (IS) | Calmar (IS) | After-Tax (OOS) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Buy-and-hold SPY | 7.2% | -55.2% | 0.164 | **10.8%** |
| Sector Momentum | 4.8% | -48.9% | 0.156 | 9.8% |
| **Dual Momentum** | **6.1%** | **-28.1%** | **0.343** | 6.5% |
| MA 10/30 SPY | 2.3% | -23.8% | 0.151 | 4.6% |

**Key findings:**

1. **Dual momentum halved max drawdown in-sample** (−28% vs −55%) with virtually no return sacrifice — the 2008 bear market filter worked perfectly

2. **Tax drag kills the edge OOS** — sector rotation beats SPY by 2% *gross* but the 37% STCG rate applied to monthly rebalancing converts that into a 1% *after-tax deficit*. High-turnover strategies need ~1.5–2× gross return to win after taxes

3. **TLT failed as a refuge in 2022** — when the Fed hiked aggressively, TLT dropped ~25% simultaneously with SPY. The "bonds are safe haven" assumption broke. **H006 will test SGOV (3-month T-bills) as refuge instead**

4. **MA crossover is strictly dominated** — slower to respond than sector rotation, worse on every metric

**George**: Yes, this is highly relevant. **LEAN** is QuantConnect's open-source backtesting and live trading engine — the same engine that runs their cloud platform, but self-hosted. Here's why it matters for us:

**Why it's better than what we built:**
- **Multi-asset**: stocks, options, futures, forex, crypto — all in one engine
- **Options support is real**: IV surfaces, Greeks, exercise assignment — our homegrown daily engine can't touch this
- **Live trading bridge**: same code backtests and goes live via broker connectors
- **Event-driven**: handles corporate actions, dividends, splits correctly out of the box
- **Python algorithms**: write strategies in Python, LEAN handles all the plumbing
- **Already has implementations** of momentum, mean-reversion, pairs trading etc.

**The tradeoff:**
- Requires Docker (heavier setup than our lightweight scripts)
- C# core means some debugging is harder
- More opinionated framework — strategies need to be structured the LEAN way

**My recommendation:** use both.

- Keep our `backtesting/daily/` scripts for **rapid hypothesis testing** — they're fast, readable, and we can tweak them in minutes
- Set up LEAN for **options strategies and live trading** — when we get to covered calls, iron condors, or volatility plays from the paper's Chapter 2, LEAN is the right tool

Want me to set up LEAN in Docker and implement one of the strategies from the paper through it? Or is the tweet giving additional context about something specific in LEAN?

**User**: Base directory for this skill: /home/node/.claude/skills/wiki

# /wiki — Personal Knowledge Wiki

The wiki is a persistent, compounding knowledge base. Raw sources are filed in
`/workspace/agent/sources/`. The wiki itself lives in `/workspace/agent/wiki/`.
You own the wiki entirely — create, update, cross-reference, synthesize.

Two special files:
- `wiki/index.md` — content catalog; read first on every query; update on every ingest
- `wiki/log.md` — append-only activity log; add an entry for every operation

---

## Operation: ingest

Triggered when the user provides a source (file path, URL, or drops a file).

**CRITICAL — process one source at a time.** If the user points at a folder or
provides multiple files, confirm before proceeding. For each source, fully complete
all wiki work before touching the next source.

### Per-source workflow

1. **Acquire full content**
   - *File in sources/*: Read it directly.
   - *URL (article/page)*: Download full content — do NOT use WebFetch (it returns
     a summary, not the full document):
     ```bash
     # HTML page
     curl -sL "URL" -o /workspace/agent/sources/filename.html
     # or for JS-rendered pages, use agent-browser if available
     
     # PDF
     curl -sL "URL" -o /workspace/agent/sources/filename.pdf
     ```
     Then Read the downloaded file.
   - *PDF already in sources/*: Read tool handles PDFs natively (up to 20 pages per
     request; use offset/limit for long documents).
   - *Transcript (text file)*: Read directly from sources/.

2. **Discuss with the user** — share key takeaways, what surprised you, what connects
   to things already in the wiki. This is the synthesis moment; don't skip it.

3. **Write/update wiki pages** — typical ingest touches 5–15 pages:
   - Summary page for this source (`wiki/sources/<slug>.md`)
   - Entity pages for people, organizations, projects mentioned
   - Concept pages for ideas, frameworks, techniques introduced
   - Comparison or synthesis pages if this source u...

# syntax=docker/dockerfile:1.7
# NanoClaw Agent Container
# Runs Claude Agent SDK in isolated Linux VM with browser automation.
#
# Runtime split:
#   - agent-runner (our TypeScript code): Bun, mounted RO at /app/src by host
#   - globally-installed Node CLIs (claude-code, agent-browser, vercel): pnpm + Node
#
# Source is never baked in — /app/src is provided by a shared read-only
# bind mount at runtime (see src/container-runner.ts). Source-only changes
# never require an image rebuild.

FROM node:22-slim

# ---- Build-time arguments ----------------------------------------------------
# CJK fonts add ~200MB. Opt in only if you render Chinese/Japanese/Korean text.
ARG INSTALL_CJK_FONTS=false

# Pin CLI versions for reproducibility. Bump deliberately — unpinned installs
# mean every rebuild silently picks up the latest and can break in lockstep
# across all users.
ARG CLAUDE_CODE_VERSION=2.1.116
ARG AGENT_BROWSER_VERSION=latest
ARG VERCEL_VERSION=latest
ARG BUN_VERSION=1.3.12

# ---- System dependencies -----------------------------------------------------
# tini: correct PID 1 / signal forwarding so outbound.db writes finalize on
# SIGTERM instead of being orphaned by the shell entrypoint.
RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/var/cache/apt,sharing=locked \
    --mount=type=cache,target=/var/lib/apt,sharing=locked \
    apt-get update &amp;&amp; apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends \
        chromium \
        fonts-liberation \
        fonts-noto-color-emoji \
        libgbm1 \
        libnss3 \
        libatk-bridge2.0-0 \
        libgtk-3-0 \
        libx11-xcb1 \
        libxcomposite1 \
        libxdamage1 \
        libxrandr2 \
        libasound2 \
        libpangocairo-1.0-0 \
        libcups2 \
        libdrm2 \
        libxshmfence1 \
        ca-certificates \
 ...

 * `groups/&lt;folder&gt;/container.json`. Mounted read-only inside the container
 * at `/workspace/agent/container.json` — the runner reads it at startup but
 * cannot modify it. Config changes go through the self-mod approval flow.
 *
 * All fields are optional — a missing file or a partial file both resolve
 * to sensible defaults. Writes are atomic-enough (write-then-rename is not
 * worth the ceremony here since there's only one writer in practice: the
 * host, from the delivery thread that processes approved system actions).
 */
import fs from 'fs';
import path from 'path';

--
function configPath(folder: string): string {
  return path.join(GROUPS_DIR, folder, 'container.json');
}

/**
 * Read the container config for a group, returning sensible defaults for
 * any missing fields (or an entirely empty config if the file is absent).
 * Never throws for missing / malformed files — corruption logs a warning
 * via console.error and falls back to empty.
 */
export function readContainerConfig(folder: string): ContainerConfig {
  const p = configPath(folder);
  if (!fs.existsSync(p)) return emptyConfig();
  try {
    const raw = JSON.parse(fs.readFileSync(p, 'utf8')) as Partial&lt;ContainerConfig&gt;;
    return {
      mcpServers: raw.mcpServers ?? {},
      packages: {
        apt: raw.packages?.apt ?? [],
        npm: raw.packages?.npm ?? [],
      },
      imageTag: raw.imageTag,
--

  fs.writeFileSync(p, JSON.stringify(config, null, 2) + '\n');
}

**User**: # Update Config Skill

Modify Claude Code configuration by updating settings.json files.

## When Hooks Are Required (Not Memory)

If the user wants something to happen automatically in response to an EVENT, they need a **hook** configured in settings.json. Memory/preferences cannot trigger automated actions.

**These require hooks:**
- "Before compacting, ask me what to preserve" → PreCompact hook
- "After writing files, run prettier" → PostToolUse hook with Write|Edit matcher
- "When I run bash commands, log them" → PreToolUse hook with Bash matcher
- "Always run tests after code changes" → PostToolUse hook

**Hook events:** PreToolUse, PostToolUse, PreCompact, PostCompact, Stop, Notification, SessionStart

## CRITICAL: Read Before Write

**Always read the existing settings file before making changes.** Merge new settings with existing ones - never replace the entire file.

## CRITICAL: Use AskUserQuestion for Ambiguity

When the user's request is ambiguous, use AskUserQuestion to clarify:
- Which settings file to modify (user/project/local)
- Whether to add to existing arrays or replace them
- Specific values when multiple options exist

## Decision: Config Tool vs Direct Edit

**Use the Config tool** for these simple settings:
- `theme`, `editorMode`, `verbose`, `model`
- `language`, `alwaysThinkingEnabled`
- `permissions.defaultMode`

**Edit settings.json directly** for:
- Hooks (PreToolUse, PostToolUse, etc.)
- Complex permission rules (allow/deny arrays)
- Environment variables
- MCP server configuration
- Plugin configuration

## Workflow

1. **Clarify intent** - Ask if the request is ambiguous
2. **Read existing file** - Use Read tool on the target settings file
3. **Merge carefully** - Preserve existing settings, especially arrays
4. **Edit file** - Use Edit tool (if file doesn't exist, ask user to create it first)
5. **Confirm** - Tell user what was changed

## Merging Arrays (Important!)

When adding to permission arrays or hook arrays, **merge with exis...

**George**: Noted and filed. Here's the summary:

**Paper**: "BSM as the Flat Limit of Information Geometry" — Bruce H. Dean, SSRN 6630259, April 2026

The core argument: BSM prices exactly on the flat σ = σ₀ slice of the Gaussian Fisher manifold of return distributions. The smile is what the market records when it wanders off that slice. Two theorems make this concrete:

**Theorem 1** — BSM is valid only when ν²T ≪ σ₀². At SPY parameters, that's roughly T < 7 days. Everything beyond a week is in the curved regime.

**Theorem 2** — The smile formula with *zero free parameters* (inputs from SPY + VIX time-series only):
- Skew: γ = ρν/(2σ₀)  
- Curvature: κ_eff = (2−3ρ²)/6 × (ν/σ₀)²  
- Bifurcation at |ρ| = √(2/3) ≈ 0.816 — smile vs. frown

**Empirical test** (April 2026, four SPY LEAPS expiries): predicted skew within 19% of observed, entirely from time-series parameters. The 19% gap is systematic — consistent with long-dated variance risk premium.

**SABR connection**: β = 1 for equity indices isn't a fitted parameter — it's a structural consequence of Čencov's theorem applied to the lognormal family.

The author has a series of related SSRN papers building toward a unified geometric theory of markets — I've noted the companion papers ([9] SSRN 6565418 on the temporal dynamics side, and a working paper on VIX-based options trading that's directly relevant). Wiki page is at `trading/algorithms/bsm-information-geometry.md`.

<message id="338" from="telegram-mg-17769" sender="Kevin Houston" time="Apr 28, 2026, 10:55 AM">some of the acronyms are confusing me. What is OOS? IBS?</message>

**George**: **OOS — Out-of-Sample**
When backtesting, you split history into two blocks: the *in-sample* (IS) period is used to develop the strategy, and the *out-of-sample* (OOS) period is kept locked away until the end as an honest reality check. If a strategy looks great IS but falls apart OOS, it was just curve-fitted to the past. OOS is the number that actually matters — it's the closest thing to live performance you can get from historical data.

**IBS — Internal Bar Strength**
A simple daily indicator: `IBS = (Close − Low) / (High − Low)`. It measures where price closed within the day's range. IBS near 0 means price closed near the day's *low* (got beaten up all day — oversold). IBS near 1 means it closed near the *high*. We use it as a mean-reversion signal: buy when IBS < threshold (e.g., 0.20) because beaten-up stocks tend to bounce the next day, sell when IBS > 0.75 because overbought stocks tend to pull back. The XLK/SMH/IGV components of the portfolio use this as a short-term trading overlay on top of the longer monthly rotation strategies.

<message id="344" from="telegram-mg-17769" sender="Kevin Houston" time="Apr 28, 2026, 9:11 PM">I want to hear more examples of tts in Lithuanian</message>

**George**: Six samples total. A few things to listen for as you go through them:

- **š** = "sh" (šalis, nesuprantu)
- **č** = "ch" (ačiū)
- **ž** = "zh" like the "s" in *measure* (graži)
- The **rolled r** in *trys* — Lithuanian keeps it crisp
- Word stress is musical and fairly consistent — long vowels get held

Lithuanian is one of the oldest surviving Indo-European languages, so some of its sounds feel almost Sanskrit-like once your ear adjusts. The daily phrases will keep building on these patterns.

**George**: This is Jane Street's "Probability & Markets" prep document — the primer they share with interview candidates. Here's what's in it and what's relevant to our work:

**What the document covers (12 pages):**
- Probability fundamentals: counting, independence, conditional probability, random variables
- Expected value: linearity (E[X+Y] = EX + EY even without independence), indicator functions, Law of Large Numbers
- **Making Markets**: bid/offer mechanics, why you buy below EV and sell above, balancing fill probability vs. expected profit
- **Adverse Selection**: the core insight — when your order fills, you should update your belief downward (someone smarter may have sold to you). "Conditional on being filled, what do I now think the EV is?"
- Problem solving: recursion for memoryless processes, edge case testing

**Direct connections to our trading work:**

The adverse selection section directly explains why our Kalshi CPI nowcasting strategy uses `immediate_or_cancel` orders — you don't want stale limit orders sitting in the book after a data revision, because everyone who hits them knows something you don't.

The confidence interval section mirrors our approach to TSMOM: we're not trying to predict exact returns, just identify when an asset's momentum is clearly positive vs. ambiguous — a soft confidence threshold.

The market-making framework (bid below EV, offer above EV, size based on how bad the loss scenario is) is essentially Kelly criterion in prose form.

**Is this for a Jane Street interview prep?** Given the interview section at the end, I'd guess you're either prepping for JS or treating it as a probability refresher. Want me to work through any of the practice problems, or was this more for general reference?

<message id="364" from="telegram-mg-17769" sender="Kevin Houston" time="Apr 29, 2026, 12:30 PM">I thought it might be useful theory behind the trading effort.</message>

**George**: Makes sense. The two most useful sections for what we're building:

**Adverse selection** — directly applicable to Kalshi market making. When we place a limit order and it gets filled immediately, that's a signal the market has information we don't. The IOC order type in the CPI strategy is the right call precisely because of this: we want to cross the spread only when our model says we have edge, not queue up as a passive target for better-informed traders.

**Expected value + Kelly** — the document's market-making framework (buy below EV, size based on worst-case loss relative to capital) is the same math as our quarter-Kelly position sizing in the Kalshi implementation. The document's warning about 1,000,000x die rolls is the practical argument for fractional Kelly — being right on EV but ruined on variance is a real failure mode.

The confidence interval section is also worth keeping in mind as we evaluate backtest results — our OOS Sharpe numbers are point estimates, and the true out-of-sample distribution has wide error bars given ~23 years of monthly data (~276 observations). Worth being calibrated about that rather than overconfident on the H116 numbers.

I'll keep the document in mind as context for the research going forward. Want to continue with H118 research tonight, or anything else on the agenda?

<message id="384" from="telegram-mg-17769" sender="Kevin Houston" time="Apr 30, 2026, 7:33 AM">Read this article
[file: THE FOUNDATIONS SERIES | FOUNDATION 4 OF 10 - ATS Trading.pdf — saved to /workspace/inbox/8520820647:870:ag-1776989405001-o24n90/THE FOUNDATIONS SERIES | FOUNDATION 4 OF 10 - ATS Trading.pdf]</message>

**George**: Good piece. A few things that connect directly to what we've been building:

**The non-ergodic point validates our test methodology.** We measure cumulative geometric return (what happens to capital on the one path through time), not average trade outcome. The dual-window test is a path test. That's the right frame for the reasons the article describes — sequence matters, and an early drawdown on limited capital does permanent damage that no later recovery fully undoes.

**H026 is literally an outlier-hunting system.** The strategy sits in BIL for long stretches — sometimes half the year — taking small opportunity costs, then captures a single massive sector trend (energy in 2022, tech in 2023-24). The 382x OOS return is driven by a handful of those big captures. The article's point about removing the 10 best trades collapsing a 20-year track record applies directly: our entire cumulative performance is concentrated in maybe 15-20 months out of 100.

**The "edge has relocated, not died" section explains our TSMOM threshold findings.** They found 20-day breakout strategies are dead but 300-day strategies recovered post-2020. We found the same gradient independently: shorter TSMOM lookbacks on sectors (H131) failed, the 12-month lookback confirmed. The horizon gradient is real and we hit it empirically.

**One thing worth challenging:** the article says short-side impairment is the only permanently structural force. For ETF rotation on the long side only (which is what H026 is), this doesn't apply — we're never short. The cyclical regime suppression (2009-2020 QE) is the relevant force for us, and they say it reverses with macro conditions. 2022 confirmed it reversed. This is good news for the strategy's durability.

The "absorbing state" section is useful to keep in mind — the case for conservative position sizing even when the system is performing. Our MaxDD of -9.6% at 100% H026 concentration is acceptable now but worth watching if the macro regime turns genuinely ...

<message id="440" from="telegram-mg-17769" sender="Kevin Houston" time="May 2, 2026, 10:27 PM">I'm not sure how to fix the KALSHI PEM key issue</message>

**George**: The issue is almost certainly that RSA private keys are multi-line, which breaks most environment variable storage. Let me explain the two possible problems and fixes:

**Problem A — Key was never saved (most common)**

If the key was generated on Kalshi's dashboard and the PEM wasn't saved at that moment, it's gone permanently. Fix: go to Kalshi → Account & Security → API Keys → delete the old key → generate a new one → copy the private key *immediately* when shown.

**Problem B — You have the key but can't store it properly**

The PEM looks like this:
```
-----BEGIN PRIVATE KEY-----
MIIEvgIBADANBgkqhkiG9w0BAQEFAASCBKgwggSkAgEAAoIBAQC...
(several more lines)
-----END PRIVATE KEY-----
```

Multi-line strings are tricky in env vars. The easiest fix: save it as a file and point to it. I can make that work with the existing scripts — they already support `KALSHI_PRIVATE_KEY_PATH`.

**What I can do right now:**

If you still have the private key PEM, paste it into a file on your machine, then upload it or paste the contents to me and I'll save it to `/workspace/agent/.kalshi_private_key.pem` (git-ignored). Then you'd only need to set one env var: `KALSHI_PRIVATE_KEY_PATH=/workspace/agent/.kalshi_private_key.pem`.

Which situation are you in — do you still have the private key, or do you need to regenerate it?

# /wiki — Personal Knowledge Wiki

The wiki is a persistent, compounding knowledge base. Raw sources are filed in
`/workspace/agent/sources/`. The wiki itself lives in `/workspace/agent/wiki/`.
You own the wiki entirely — create, update, cross-reference, synthesize.

Two special files:
- `wiki/index.md` — content catalog; read first on every query; update on every ingest
- `wiki/log.md` — append-only activity log; add an entry for every operation

---

## Operation: ingest

Triggered when the user provides a source (file path, URL, or drops a file).

**CRITICAL — process one source at a time.** If the user points at a folder or
provides multiple files, confirm before proceeding. For each source, fully complete
all wiki work before touching the next source.

### Per-source workflow

1. **Acquire full content**
   - *File in sources/*: Read it directly.
   - *URL (article/page)*: Download full content — do NOT use WebFetch (it returns
     a summary, not the full document):
     ```bash
     # HTML page
     curl -sL "URL" -o /workspace/agent/sources/filename.html
     # or for JS-rendered pages, use agent-browser if available
     
     # PDF
     curl -sL "URL" -o /workspace/agent/sources/filename.pdf
     ```
     Then Read the downloaded file.
   - *PDF already in sources/*: Read tool handles PDFs natively (up to 20 pages per
     request; use offset/limit for long documents).
   - *Transcript (text file)*: Read directly from sources/.

2. **Discuss with the user** — share key takeaways, what surprised you, what connects
   to things already in the wiki. This is the synthesis moment; don't skip it.

3. **Write/update wiki pages** — typical ingest touches 5–15 pages:
   - Summary page for this source (`wiki/sources/<slug>.md`)
   - Entity pages for people, organizations, projects mentioned
   - Concept pages for ideas, frameworks, techniques introduced
   - Comparison or synthesis pages if this source u...

# Browser Automation with agent-browser

## Quick start

```bash
agent-browser open <url>        # Navigate to page
agent-browser snapshot -i       # Get interactive elements with refs
agent-browser click @e1         # Click element by ref
agent-browser fill @e2 "text"   # Fill input by ref
agent-browser close             # Close browser
```

## Core workflow

1. Navigate: `agent-browser open <url>`
2. Snapshot: `agent-browser snapshot -i` (returns elements with refs like `@e1`, `@e2`)
3. Interact using refs from the snapshot
4. Re-snapshot after navigation or significant DOM changes

## Commands

### Navigation

```bash
agent-browser open <url>      # Navigate to URL
agent-browser back            # Go back
agent-browser forward         # Go forward
agent-browser reload          # Reload page
agent-browser close           # Close browser
```

### Snapshot (page analysis)

```bash
agent-browser snapshot            # Full accessibility tree
agent-browser snapshot -i         # Interactive elements only (recommended)
agent-browser snapshot -c         # Compact output
agent-browser snapshot -d 3       # Limit depth to 3
agent-browser snapshot -s "#main" # Scope to CSS selector
```

### Interactions (use @refs from snapshot)

```bash
agent-browser click @e1           # Click
agent-browser dblclick @e1        # Double-click
agent-browser fill @e2 "text"     # Clear and type
agent-browser type @e2 "text"     # Type without clearing
agent-browser press Enter         # Press key
agent-browser hover @e1           # Hover
agent-browser check @e1           # Check checkbox
agent-browser uncheck @e1         # Uncheck checkbox
agent-browser select @e1 "value"  # Select dropdown option
agent-browser scroll down 500     # Scroll page
agent-browser upload @e1 file.pdf # Upload files
```

### Get information

```bash
agent-browser get text @e1        # Get element text
agent-browser get html @e1        # Get inner...

- **Black-Scholes call pricing**: `C = S·N(d1) − K·e^(−rT)·N(d2)` with d1 = (ln(S/K) + (r + 0.5σ²)T) / (σ√T)

- **Exit-day P&L model**: All trade P&L assigned to exit date (not daily MTM) — inflates Sharpe/CAGR, consistent with H161
- **Covered call economics**: Cap upside at K = S × (1 + OTM_strike); improves win rate but reduces mean return when stocks frequently exceed strike

**George**: 977 stars, actively maintained, last commit today. Here's the rundown:

**What it is**: End-to-end Python trading framework — strategy → backtest → live deployment, with the same code running across all three environments.

**Architecture**: Strategies inherit from `TradingStrategy`, define intervals/symbols as class attributes, implement `run_strategy(context, data)`. Clean, consistent API whether you're backtesting or running live.

**Two-engine backtesting**:
- *Vector* (Polars-powered) — fast parameter grid sweeps, multi-window robustness testing, runs thousands of configs in seconds
- *Event-driven* — bar-by-bar simulation with slippage/fill models, blotter tracking, partial fills
- 30+ metrics built in: Sharpe, Sortino, Calmar, VaR, CVaR, drawdown, Monte Carlo, permutation testing

**Data/exchanges**: CCXT (70+ exchanges — Binance, Kraken, Coinbase etc.), yfinance, Alpha Vantage, Polygon, custom CSV/Parquet

**Reporting**: Self-contained HTML dashboards with equity curves, monthly heatmaps, benchmark comparisons. Also ships a built-in **MCP server** for AI agent integration (Claude/Copilot can query strategy results directly).

**Deployment**: Local, AWS Lambda, Azure Functions — live trading via CCXT with portfolio persistence and state recovery on restart.

**Stack**: Python 3.10+, Polars, Flask, SQLAlchemy, CCXT, Plotly. Apache 2.0.

**Relevance to our work**: Primarily crypto-focused via CCXT, but the data layer supports yfinance/Polygon for equities. The dual-backtest pattern (vector screening → event-driven validation) mirrors what we're doing manually. The MCP server is interesting — could theoretically hook into research sessions. Not a direct fit for PEAD/NLP work since our edge is in the signal generation layer, not the execution framework, but worth keeping an eye on.

# Browser Automation with agent-browser

## Quick start

```bash
agent-browser open <url>        # Navigate to page
agent-browser snapshot -i       # Get interactive elements with refs
agent-browser click @e1         # Click element by ref
agent-browser fill @e2 "text"   # Fill input by ref
agent-browser close             # Close browser
```

## Core workflow

1. Navigate: `agent-browser open <url>`
2. Snapshot: `agent-browser snapshot -i` (returns elements with refs like `@e1`, `@e2`)
3. Interact using refs from the snapshot
4. Re-snapshot after navigation or significant DOM changes

## Commands

### Navigation

```bash
agent-browser open <url>      # Navigate to URL
agent-browser back            # Go back
agent-browser forward         # Go forward
agent-browser reload          # Reload page
agent-browser close           # Close browser
```

### Snapshot (page analysis)

```bash
agent-browser snapshot            # Full accessibility tree
agent-browser snapshot -i         # Interactive elements only (recommended)
agent-browser snapshot -c         # Compact output
agent-browser snapshot -d 3       # Limit depth to 3
agent-browser snapshot -s "#main" # Scope to CSS selector
```

### Interactions (use @refs from snapshot)

```bash
agent-browser click @e1           # Click
agent-browser dblclick @e1        # Double-click
agent-browser fill @e2 "text"     # Clear and type
agent-browser type @e2 "text"     # Type without clearing
agent-browser press Enter         # Press key
agent-browser hover @e1           # Hover
agent-browser check @e1           # Check checkbox
agent-browser uncheck @e1         # Uncheck checkbox
agent-browser select @e1 "value"  # Select dropdown option
agent-browser scroll down 500     # Scroll page
agent-browser upload @e1 file.pdf # Upload files
```

### Get information

```bash
agent-browser get text @e1        # Get element text
agent-browser get html @e1        # Get inner...

# /wiki — Personal Knowledge Wiki

The wiki is a persistent, compounding knowledge base. Raw sources are filed in
`/workspace/agent/sources/`. The wiki itself lives in `/workspace/agent/wiki/`.
You own the wiki entirely — create, update, cross-reference, synthesize.

Two special files:
- `wiki/index.md` — content catalog; read first on every query; update on every ingest
- `wiki/log.md` — append-only activity log; add an entry for every operation

---

## Operation: ingest

Triggered when the user provides a source (file path, URL, or drops a file).

**CRITICAL — process one source at a time.** If the user points at a folder or
provides multiple files, confirm before proceeding. For each source, fully complete
all wiki work before touching the next source.

### Per-source workflow

1. **Acquire full content**
   - *File in sources/*: Read it directly.
   - *URL (article/page)*: Download full content — do NOT use WebFetch (it returns
     a summary, not the full document):
     ```bash
     # HTML page
     curl -sL "URL" -o /workspace/agent/sources/filename.html
     # or for JS-rendered pages, use agent-browser if available
     
     # PDF
     curl -sL "URL" -o /workspace/agent/sources/filename.pdf
     ```
     Then Read the downloaded file.
   - *PDF already in sources/*: Read tool handles PDFs natively (up to 20 pages per
     request; use offset/limit for long documents).
   - *Transcript (text file)*: Read directly from sources/.

2. **Discuss with the user** — share key takeaways, what surprised you, what connects
   to things already in the wiki. This is the synthesis moment; don't skip it.

3. **Write/update wiki pages** — typical ingest touches 5–15 pages:
   - Summary page for this source (`wiki/sources/<slug>.md`)
   - Entity pages for people, organizations, projects mentioned
   - Concept pages for ideas, frameworks, techniques introduced
   - Comparison or synthesis pages if this source u...

**Paper:** *A Portfolio Approach to Impact Investment* — J.P. Morgan Social Finance, Oct 2012 (Yasemin Saltuk & Ali El Idrissi). 48 pages. PDF saved to `sources/`.

**Core idea:** Extends Markowitz's two-axis (risk/return) framework to three dimensions by adding **impact** as a co-equal axis. Each investment is a triangle on Impact / Return / Risk. Individual investments don't need to hit all targets — the aggregate portfolio does. Can expand to six dimensions (splitting each axis into two sub-components) for finer-grained analysis.

**Key stance worth noting:** They explicitly argue against the "impact requires sacrificing return" debate — instead, evaluate each deal's economics on its own merits. Asset-class generalizations are unhelpful.

**5 wiki pages created:** source summary, concept pages for impact investing + the 3D framework, people page for Saltuk, org page for J.P. Morgan Social Finance.

**George**: <message to="telegram-mg-17769">
Had a good look at it. QuantDinger is a self-hosted quantitative trading *platform* — a full-stack app for building, backtesting, and executing your own strategies. It's not a pre-built algo or signal service.

**What it actually is:**
- Web IDE where you write Python indicators/strategies
- Backtesting engine (multi-timeframe, commissions/slippage modeled, equity curve tracking)
- Live execution via 10+ crypto exchanges (CCXT), IBKR for stocks, MT5 for forex
- LLM integration (OpenAI/OpenRouter) to help generate strategy code
- Self-hosted via Docker Compose

**Code quality:** Solid. 6,300+ lines in the core backtest engine, proper error handling, audit logging, security defaults (paper-only unless you explicitly flip to live). Active development — last commit was today. One of the noted contributors is Claude Code, so it was built with AI assistance.

**Caveats to know:**
- Annualization formula is simplified (total return ÷ years, not true CAGR) — overstates annual return for multi-year runs
- No survivorship bias filtering for equity backtests — users need to handle that manually
- Cross-sectional/multi-asset strategies not fully wired into the live trading path yet (crypto single-instrument is the mature use case)
- Makes no performance claims — it's a tool, you supply the alpha

**Relationship to our work:** It could run any of our hypotheses (H026 sector rotation, H163 PEAD-NLP, H181 reversal) if you wanted a front-end dashboard. But our current setup (custom Python + Alpaca paper trading) is more tightly controlled for the backtesting we're doing. The FinBERT/8-K pipeline doesn't slot in trivially.

What angle are you thinking — use it as infrastructure, or just a reference?
</message>

**User**: Base directory for this skill: /home/node/.claude/skills/self-customize

# Self-Customization

You can modify your own environment. Different kinds of changes have different workflows.

## Decision Tree

**What needs to change?**

- **`CLAUDE.local.md` or files in your workspace** → Edit directly, no approval needed. Your workspace (`/workspace/agent/`) is persisted on the host. (Note: the composed `CLAUDE.md` itself is read-only and regenerated every spawn — write to `CLAUDE.local.md` instead.)
- **System package (apt) or global npm package** → `install_packages`. Requires admin approval. On approval, image rebuild + container restart happen automatically.
- **MCP server** → `add_mcp_server`. Requires admin approval. On approval, container restarts with the new server wired up (no rebuild — bun runs TS directly).
- **Your source code or Dockerfile** → Delegate to a builder agent via `create_agent` (see below).
- **A new specialist capability** → `create_agent` to spin up a dedicated agent for it.

## Workflow: Code Changes via Builder Agent

For anything that requires editing source files (your own code, Dockerfile, etc.), **do not edit directly** — delegate to a builder agent. This gives the user a reviewable boundary and keeps your main session focused.

1. Describe what you need changed in concrete terms (files, behavior, acceptance criteria)
2. Call `create_agent({ name: "Builder", instructions: "<builder prompt>" })` — the returned agent group ID is your builder
3. Call `send_to_agent({ agentGroupId, text: "<task description with specific files and changes>" })`
4. The builder works in its own container, makes the changes, and reports back
5. You review the builder's summary and confirm with the user. Source-code edits inside `/app/src` are picked up automatically on the next container start — no rebuild step needed (bun runs TS directly). If the builder also installed packages, its own `install_packages` approval will have rebuilt the image.

### Builder Agen...

My scheduled task modules accumulate failure history (PEAD EDGAR User-Agent error, podcast email auth, here.now jq missing) but that knowledge lives in comments or commit messages, not in the skill instructions where future runs can use it. **Less applicable:**

- **Evals before writing** — Most of my tasks are already operational, so this applies mainly when I add new scheduled tasks going forward.

**What:** A `task-registry.md` loaded every session (via `@` import in `CLAUDE.local.md`). One section per recurring task — trigger, success criteria, and a **gotchas block**.

**The compound angle:** The protocol is to append to the gotchas block whenever a task fails and gets fixed — same commit as the fix. Over time, the registry becomes a compressed memory of everything that can go wrong, always in context. The Perplexity insight is that negative examples ("never do X when Y") are more token-efficient and behaviorally effective than positive instructions — you're encoding real failure modes, not hypothetical workflows.

Today's token cost: ~2,000 tokens per session. Payoff: every failure I've already solved stays solved permanently.</message>

# Browser Automation with agent-browser

## Quick start

```bash
agent-browser open <url>        # Navigate to page
agent-browser snapshot -i       # Get interactive elements with refs
agent-browser click @e1         # Click element by ref
agent-browser fill @e2 "text"   # Fill input by ref
agent-browser close             # Close browser
```

## Core workflow

1. Navigate: `agent-browser open <url>`
2. Snapshot: `agent-browser snapshot -i` (returns elements with refs like `@e1`, `@e2`)
3. Interact using refs from the snapshot
4. Re-snapshot after navigation or significant DOM changes

## Commands

### Navigation

```bash
agent-browser open <url>      # Navigate to URL
agent-browser back            # Go back
agent-browser forward         # Go forward
agent-browser reload          # Reload page
agent-browser close           # Close browser
```

### Snapshot (page analysis)

```bash
agent-browser snapshot            # Full accessibility tree
agent-browser snapshot -i         # Interactive elements only (recommended)
agent-browser snapshot -c         # Compact output
agent-browser snapshot -d 3       # Limit depth to 3
agent-browser snapshot -s "#main" # Scope to CSS selector
```

### Interactions (use @refs from snapshot)

```bash
agent-browser click @e1           # Click
agent-browser dblclick @e1        # Double-click
agent-browser fill @e2 "text"     # Clear and type
agent-browser type @e2 "text"     # Type without clearing
agent-browser press Enter         # Press key
agent-browser hover @e1           # Hover
agent-browser check @e1           # Check checkbox
agent-browser uncheck @e1         # Uncheck checkbox
agent-browser select @e1 "value"  # Select dropdown option
agent-browser scroll down 500     # Scroll page
agent-browser upload @e1 file.pdf # Upload files
```

### Get information

```bash
agent-browser get text @e1        # Get element text
agent-browser get html @e1        # Get inner...

<message to="telegram-mg-17769">Read it — the full article, 5.9M views. Here's the thesis and where we can apply it.

**The argument:** HTML is a better output format than markdown for agent work because:
- It can express things markdown can't without ASCII hacks: color-coded tables, charts, timelines, interactive elements
- Long markdown (100+ lines) doesn't get read. HTML with tabs and navigation does.
- It's shareable — upload and send a link. Markdown requires a renderer.
- Two-way interaction: HTML can have sliders/knobs that export back to a Claude prompt ("copy as prompt" button)

**Concrete applications for us, in priority order:**

**1. Nightly research summaries → HTML + here.now link**
Right now when the nightly agent finishes, you get a wall of text in Telegram. Instead: a single HTML page summarizing the session (hypotheses confirmed/queued, dream cycle proposals, wiki additions) published to here.now. You'd get a URL, click it, and see a visual recap instead of reading a paragraph dump.

**2. Hypothesis log → HTML navigator**
The hypothesis log is 60+ dense entries in a single markdown file. As HTML it could be a table with color-coded status (green=CONFIRMED, red=NOT CONFIRMED, blue=QUEUED), sortable by Sharpe/CAGR/date, collapsible detail sections. Much more useful as a reference document.

**3. Backtesting results → HTML instead of .txt**
Results like `h181_industry_reversal.txt` are plain text tables. An HTML version could include the IS/OOS comparison chart, a sparkline of the equity curve, and a color-coded pass/fail against confirmation criteria.

**4. Custom editing interface for portfolio allocation**
Thariq's "custom editing interface" pattern is very applicable: build a throwaway HTML page where you drag sliders for H026/H041a/H045 weights, see the ...

---

**Intraday PEAD Scanner**

The overnight pass runs once at 11 PM and misses anything filed during market hours — pre-market earnings at 6 AM, mid-day 8-Ks, etc. The fix is a polling task that checks EDGAR's live 8-K feed every 30 minutes during market hours and wakes me only when something qualifying appears.

**How it works:**

```
Pre-task script (every 30 min, 6 AM–5 PM CT, weekdays):
  1. Poll EDGAR RSS: new 8-K filings in the last 35 minutes
  2. Filter for Item 2.02 (Results of Operations = earnings)
  3. Compare against a cursor file (last-seen accession number)
  4. Return wakeAgent: true only if new earnings 8-Ks found
```

When woken:
- Run FinBERT on the transcript/press release (H163/H174 pipeline)
- Check entry thresholds (score ≥ 0.18, surprise ≥ 0.02)
- If qualifying → submit order immediately via Alpaca paper API
- Skip if ticker already in open positions

**State file:** `pead_intraday_cursor.json` — tracks last accession seen so the script doesn't re-process old filings on each poll.

**Cost:** ~22 script checks/day. On most windows there are zero new earnings 8-Ks, so `wakeAgent: false` and no API credits used. Real wakeups: maybe 1–3/day during heavy earnings weeks, essentially zero outside earnings season.

**What it adds:** Filings from 6–9:30 AM get caught and orders placed at open (same execution path as the existing open pass). Filings during market hours get orders submitted immediately rather than waiting until next morning.

---

Want me to build it? I'd need to write:
1. `pead_intraday_scanner.py` — the FinBERT scoring + order submission logic
2. The pre-task script (Node.js, polls EDGAR RSS)
3. The scheduled task (30 min recurrence, market hours only)
</message>

<message id="680" from="telegram-mg-17769" sender="Kevin Houston" time="May 9, 2026, 9:22 PM">Does this look interesting https://github.com/tinyhumansai/openhuman</message>

**George**: <message to="telegram-mg-17769">
Interesting for a few specific reasons, but not as a product to adopt.

**What it is:** Open-source desktop AI assistant (Rust + TypeScript, GPL-3.0, 549 stars, early beta). Has a desktop mascot, can join Google Meets, 118 OAuth integrations, token compression layer.

**The parts worth studying:**

1. **Auto-fetch loop** — every 20 minutes it pulls Gmail, Calendar, GitHub, Slack, etc. into a memory tree automatically. No prompts. By morning the agent already has tomorrow's context. That's a more aggressive version of what the dream cycle does nightly — continuous ingestion vs. batch.

2. **Memory Tree architecture** — everything ingested gets canonicalized into ≤3k-token Markdown chunks, scored for relevance, folded into hierarchical summary trees, stored in SQLite, and mirrored as .md files in an Obsidian-compatible vault. Karpathy-style. Much more structured than the flat wiki you have now.

3. **TokenJuice** — compresses every tool call result, scraped page, and email body before it touches an LLM. HTML→Markdown, URL shortening. Claims up to 80% token reduction. That concept is sound and applies to how I summarize tool outputs.

**What's not relevant for you:** The desktop mascot, Google Meet face, OAuth installer, and "no terminal required" pitch — you don't need any of that. Your OneCLI proxy already handles credentials better than their OAuth layer. The GPL-3.0 license is also restrictive if you ever want to build on it.

**Honest take:** The product is too early and too generic to replace what you have. But the Memory Tree pattern — continuous auto-ingestion with chunk scoring into hierarchical SQLite-backed summaries — is a more mature version of the wiki system. Worth noting as a design direction if you ever want to replace the flat wiki with something that self-organizes. Want me to save it?
</message>

<message id="690" from="telegram-mg-17769" sender="Kevin Houston" time="May 9, 2026, 9:47 PM">Look into this https://x.com/anthonysandford/status/2052798099521671327?s=46&amp;t=wWBOACRgUcr7qZ6UHCRiXA 

Is there a way to detect unusual options activity and profit from it?</message>

**George**: <message to="telegram-mg-17769">
The tweet is @AnthonySandford showing $INTC — large aggressive call buys ("ask side sweeps") on the 5/15 $135c at open and again 45 minutes before a news drop. Both trades 100%+. He's referencing the **Unusual Whales** platform.

**Does it work? The honest picture:**

The signal is real — there's solid academic evidence (Pan & Poteshman 2006, multiple follow-ups) that abnormal options volume before catalysts is genuinely predictive. Put/call ratio changes predict near-term returns. Informed traders do show up in options before earnings surprises and M&A announcements.

But there are three hard problems:

1. **Data latency.** Retail options feeds are 15-20 minutes delayed. By the time you see the sweep, the smart money is already in. Professional OPRA feeds cost $1k+/month. The tweet example looks clean in retrospect; live execution is much messier.

2. **Noise.** ~95% of "unusual" volume is covered calls, portfolio hedges, market-maker inventory. The signal you actually want — informed directional bets — is buried in noise. Most UOA services have a very high false positive rate.

3. **Crowding.** Unusual Whales, Cheddar Flow, Market Chameleon, SpotGamma all publish this in near real-time to hundreds of thousands of subscribers. When everyone sees the same alert at the same time, the edge compresses fast.

**What I'd recommend:**

This is a legitimate hypothesis worth testing, specifically framed as: *does following ask-side call sweeps (volume > 5× OI, aggressive buy, near-term expiry) on large-cap earnings names generate positive 3-5 day equity returns?*

The strongest version combines it with what we already do — use UOA as a **pre-earnings filter** for the PEAD pipeline. If a stock shows unusual call buying 1-3 days before an earnings 8-K, weight the PEAD signal higher.

**What we'd need:**
- Options flow data with ask/bid classification — Polygon's options plan (~$200/month) covers this, or Unusual Whales has an API tier
- A back...

**My read:** there are real techniques in there (LSTM + HMM regime detection, stationary features, walk-forward validation) but the framing is pure hype:
- "83% return" with no timeframe cited — if that's 5 years it's ~13%/year, worse than SPY
- No Sharpe, no drawdown stats

**The kernel worth taking seriously:** walk-forward LSTM + HMM on prediction market probabilities. The HMM part is genuinely useful — it detects regime shifts in a market's probability time-series (e.g., a Kalshi contract going from pricing 30% to drifting toward 60%). LSTM on top captures sequence patterns in how those regimes evolve.

**Connection to H185:** our queued Kalshi hypothesis uses LLM consensus (10-persona swarm) for nowcasting CPI/Fed events. This tweet is about a different angle — predicting how market prices themselves will move rather than forecasting the underlying event. They're complementary: H185 answers "what's the true probability?" while LSTM+HMM would answer "is this contract mis-priced relative to its recent trajectory?"

<message id="698" from="telegram-mg-17769" sender="Kevin Houston" time="May 9, 2026, 11:26 PM">What about this https://x.com/milesdeutscher/status/2053067499240071459?s=46&amp;t=wWBOACRgUcr7qZ6UHCRiXA</message>

**George**: <message to="telegram-mg-17769">
Miles Deutscher is a crypto influencer. The tweet is a step-by-step guide to "vibe-coding" TradingView indicators with Claude Code — his signals printed +190%, +95%, +85% returns.

**Short answer: no, this isn't anything.**

The specific problem is TradingView backtesting. When you write a Pine Script indicator and look at the chart, every signal marker appears on historical candles and the returns look real — but Pine Script's default backtesting engine has no protection against look-ahead bias. You can accidentally reference future candles, and even when you don't, the strategy is fit to the same history it was "tested" on. +190% on a crypto chart in a given window is trivially achievable by any momentum signal during a bull run. There's no IS/OOS split, no statistical significance, no out-of-sample period.

This is the same pattern we've seen across the research log: IS numbers that look great, OOS where the signal collapses (see H156, H157, H159 — all had impressive IS Sharpes and degraded hard OOS).

**The grain of truth:** the Claude Code + TradingView MCP workflow for rapid indicator prototyping is real and could save time on visual exploration. If you ever want to quickly eyeball a chart pattern, having Claude write Pine Script is genuinely faster than doing it by hand. That's a useful development tool, not a trading edge.

What we have is more rigorous than anything in that thread — explicit walk-forward, hold-out OOS periods, Sharpe + MaxDD confirmation criteria before anything touches paper trading.
</message>

<message id="704" from="telegram-mg-17769" sender="Kevin Houston" time="May 9, 2026, 11:34 PM">Any ideas here worth stealing https://x.com/the_smart_ape/status/2053034897514660074?s=46&amp;t=wWBOACRgUcr7qZ6UHCRiXA</message>

**George**: <message to="telegram-mg-17769">
It's @the_smart_ape riffing on the @trq212 "HTML is the new markdown" thread, adding a 3-question decision framework:

1. **Audience** — will Claude re-read this, or just humans?
2. **Lifecycle** — edited repeatedly, or written once?
3. **Horizon** — lives a day, a quarter, or forever?

The rules that fall out: anything Claude re-ingests (wiki, CLAUDE.md, hypothesis log) → MD, no exceptions (HTML costs 3× the tokens, degrades RAG relevance 15-25%). Anything edited more than 2-3 times → MD ("markup drift" — Claude rewrites surrounding boilerplate on every edit, invisible content rot). One-shot human deliverable → HTML is fine.

**The one pattern worth stealing:** "one MD source, many HTML renders on demand." You have one canonical `architecture.md` — a 10-line script pipes it through Claude with three different system prompts to produce exec view, engineering view, onboarding view. Each HTML is a derived artifact; regenerate when the source changes.

We already do this correctly — the session summaries and hypothesis log HTML are generated from MD sources and discarded. The wiki and hypothesis log stay MD. The only thing worth formalizing is making the HTML dashboard pages explicitly derived from their MD sources rather than living as primary documents. Worth keeping in mind for the next dashboard rebuild, but nothing to do right now.
</message>

# /wiki — Personal Knowledge Wiki

The wiki is a persistent, compounding knowledge base. Raw sources are filed in
`/workspace/agent/sources/`. The wiki itself lives in `/workspace/agent/wiki/`.
You own the wiki entirely — create, update, cross-reference, synthesize.

Two special files:
- `wiki/index.md` — content catalog; read first on every query; update on every ingest
- `wiki/log.md` — append-only activity log; add an entry for every operation

---

## Operation: ingest

Triggered when the user provides a source (file path, URL, or drops a file).

**CRITICAL — process one source at a time.** If the user points at a folder or
provides multiple files, confirm before proceeding. For each source, fully complete
all wiki work before touching the next source.

### Per-source workflow

1. **Acquire full content**
   - *File in sources/*: Read it directly.
   - *URL (article/page)*: Download full content — do NOT use WebFetch (it returns
     a summary, not the full document):
     ```bash
     # HTML page
     curl -sL "URL" -o /workspace/agent/sources/filename.html
     # or for JS-rendered pages, use agent-browser if available
     
     # PDF
     curl -sL "URL" -o /workspace/agent/sources/filename.pdf
     ```
     Then Read the downloaded file.
   - *PDF already in sources/*: Read tool handles PDFs natively (up to 20 pages per
     request; use offset/limit for long documents).
   - *Transcript (text file)*: Read directly from sources/.

2. **Discuss with the user** — share key takeaways, what surprised you, what connects
   to things already in the wiki. This is the synthesis moment; don't skip it.

3. **Write/update wiki pages** — typical ingest touches 5–15 pages:
   - Summary page for this source (`wiki/sources/<slug>.md`)
   - Entity pages for people, organizations, projects mentioned
   - Concept pages for ideas, frameworks, techniques introduced
   - Comparison or synthesis pages if this source u...

# Browser Automation with agent-browser

## Quick start

```bash
agent-browser open <url>        # Navigate to page
agent-browser snapshot -i       # Get interactive elements with refs
agent-browser click @e1         # Click element by ref
agent-browser fill @e2 "text"   # Fill input by ref
agent-browser close             # Close browser
```

## Core workflow

1. Navigate: `agent-browser open <url>`
2. Snapshot: `agent-browser snapshot -i` (returns elements with refs like `@e1`, `@e2`)
3. Interact using refs from the snapshot
4. Re-snapshot after navigation or significant DOM changes

## Commands

### Navigation

```bash
agent-browser open <url>      # Navigate to URL
agent-browser back            # Go back
agent-browser forward         # Go forward
agent-browser reload          # Reload page
agent-browser close           # Close browser
```

### Snapshot (page analysis)

```bash
agent-browser snapshot            # Full accessibility tree
agent-browser snapshot -i         # Interactive elements only (recommended)
agent-browser snapshot -c         # Compact output
agent-browser snapshot -d 3       # Limit depth to 3
agent-browser snapshot -s "#main" # Scope to CSS selector
```

### Interactions (use @refs from snapshot)

```bash
agent-browser click @e1           # Click
agent-browser dblclick @e1        # Double-click
agent-browser fill @e2 "text"     # Clear and type
agent-browser type @e2 "text"     # Type without clearing
agent-browser press Enter         # Press key
agent-browser hover @e1           # Hover
agent-browser check @e1           # Check checkbox
agent-browser uncheck @e1         # Uncheck checkbox
agent-browser select @e1 "value"  # Select dropdown option
agent-browser scroll down 500     # Scroll page
agent-browser upload @e1 file.pdf # Upload files
```

### Get information

```bash
agent-browser get text @e1        # Get element text
agent-browser get html @e1        # Get inner...

**Nightly research — H192 CONFIRMED**

**PEAD overnight:** No earnings tonight, watchlist empty again.

---

**H192 — Betting Against Beta (BAB) on 30-Stock Universe**

All 4 variants confirmed — all beat SPY OOS (Sharpe 0.954, Cumul 2.04×):

| Variant | OOS Sharpe | OOS Cumul | MaxDD |
|---------|-----------|-----------|-------|
| A: Raw 1yr beta | 0.962 | 2.067× | −17.2% |
| B: Vasicek-shrunk (0.6/0.4) | 0.962 | 2.067× | −17.2% |
| C: Low-beta + low-vol 50/50 | 1.098 | 2.164× | −11.7% |
| **D: Sector-neutral beta** | **1.367** | **2.518×** | **−17.1%** |

**The interesting twist:** BAB and Low-Vol (H191) are nearly the same signal on large-cap stocks. Corr(H192-A, H191-A) OOS = 0.799 — both methods rank the same defensive names (JNJ, WMT, COST, IBM). Vasicek shrinkage (B) makes zero difference on only 30 stocks — the relative rankings are unchanged.

**Sector-neutral beta (D) is the outlier:** Sharpe 1.367 is the highest in this entire family (beats H191-C's 1.110, H190's 1.191). Why? Absolute beta just selects defensive sectors universally. Sector-neutral beta finds the "calmest stock within each sector" — it picks from tech, financials, energy too — giving much better diversification.

**Portfolio implication:** H192 confirms BAB works on this universe, but it's too correlated with H191 to deploy as a second satellite (Corr = 0.72–0.94 depending on variant). The production satellite recommendation stays: H181 reversal (primary) with H190 blend implementation. H191-A adds diversification to H181 (Corr = 0.342). H192 doesn't add enough on top of that.

**H193 queued:** test H192-D (sector-neutral BAB, Sharpe 1.367) + H181 reversal blend — sector-neutral beta may complement reversal if their stock selections differ meaningfully.
</message>

**Overnight report — 2026-05-13**

**PEAD (H174):** No earnings events tonight. Watchlist cleared.

**Nightly research: 2 hypotheses run**

**H193 — BAB + Reversal blend (NOT CONFIRMED)**
Tested blending H192-D sector-neutral BAB (OOS Sharpe 1.367) with H181 industry reversal (1.138) at 7 weight ratios. Key finding: the two strategies pick almost entirely different stocks (only 14% average overlap) — their signals are nearly orthogonal in stock selection space. But no blend beats H192-D standalone on Sharpe. The 80/20 blend does achieve the best drawdown (-12.7% vs H192-D's -16.4%).

Bottom line: H181 remains the right satellite for paper trading — higher CAGR (24.6% vs 19.1%) even with slightly lower Sharpe. H192-D is the better risk-adjusted choice if drawdown control is the priority.

**H196 — STORM on 90-stock universe (NOT CONFIRMED)**
Tested whether expanding the STORM dual VQ-VAE from 30 to 90 stocks would unlock the scale advantage hypothesized in H195. The opposite happened: OOS Sharpe collapsed to 0.528 (vs H195's 0.963), failing to beat SPY (0.573). IS/OOS degradation worsened from 41% to 65%. The larger graph (8,100 potential edges vs 900) is too noisy for 84 months of training data.

**STORM research line closed.** It works at 30-stock scale (Sharpe 0.963) but underperforms H192-D (1.367), and scale doesn't help. The architecture appears market-condition-specific to the original paper.

---

<message id="1032" from="telegram-mg-17769" sender="Kevin Houston" time="May 14, 2026, 10:12 PM">What are the various backtesting tools we've looked into?</message>

**George**: <message to="telegram-mg-17769">Four tools in the wiki, plus our own homegrown setup:

**1. Homegrown (`backtesting/daily/`)**
Our primary workhorse. Custom Python scripts using pandas/yfinance for daily EOD equity hypothesis testing. Fast iteration — all the H-series tests ran here. No options support.

**2. Vectorbt**
Fully vectorized (NumPy/Numba) — ~1000x faster than event-driven frameworks for parameter sweeps. Best for ML strategies and optimization runs. Research-only, no live trading path. Apache 2.0 but the free tier has a Commons Clause (non-commercial restriction). v1.0.0 dropped April 2026.

**3. Backtrader**
Event-driven, bar-by-bar — mirrors live execution closely. Has a working Alpaca integration bridge (`alpaca-backtrader-api`). Good for strategies you want to paper/live trade without rewriting. Original repo is unmaintained; use the `backtrader2` fork. Beginner-friendly.

**4. LEAN / QuantConnect**
The serious options backtesting tool. Full Greeks, multi-leg combo orders, early assignment modeling. Same engine as QuantConnect's cloud platform. Available self-hosted via Docker (approval was pending) or free tier cloud (10 backtests/day). Marked as the right choice when we move options strategies into a proper backtest. CLI is installed but Docker wasn't set up yet.

**5. Qlib (Microsoft)**
AI-oriented end-to-end quant platform — data ingestion → feature engineering → ML model training → backtest → live serving, all in one config. 41k stars, actively maintained. Marked as the candidate framework for Phase 2 ML strategies. Not integrated yet.

---
Short version: homegrown for daily equity, Vectorbt for param sweeps, Backtrader for live trading wiring, LEAN for options, Qlib for ML models.</message>

**George**: <message to="telegram-mg-17769">
**Twelve Data** — official Python client for their financial data API. Here's the breakdown:

**What it provides:**
- OHLC time series — stocks, forex, crypto, ETFs, indices
- Fundamentals: earnings calendars, income/balance/cash flow statements, insider transactions, institutional holders, IPO calendars
- 100+ built-in server-side technical indicators
- Real-time WebSocket streaming (Pro plan only)
- Pre/post-market data

**Pricing:**
| Tier | Cost | Limits |
|------|------|--------|
| Free | $0 | 800 calls/day, 8/min — US equities, forex, crypto |
| Grow | $79/mo | More credits, 70+ global markets |
| Pro | $149–229/mo | WebSocket, real-time EU data, fundamentals add-ons |
| Ultra | $329+/mo | All markets, India, mutual funds |

**How it stacks up against what we already have:**

| Need | Current | Twelve Data adds |
|------|---------|-----------------|
| EOD prices | Polygon free / yfinance | ≈ same on free tier |
| Fundamentals | FMP (`$FMP_API_KEY`) | Overlaps; similar coverage |
| Earnings calendars | FMP | Overlap — TD might be cleaner API |
| Technical indicators | We compute manually | Pre-computed server-side — saves dev time |
| Real-time streaming | Alpaca (paper/live) | Only on Pro+ |
| WebSocket | Alpaca | Only on Pro+ |

**My read:** The free tier is useful but not transformative — we have most of the data covered between Polygon, FMP, and Alpaca. The **one genuine differentiator** at the free tier is the 100+ server-side technical indicators and the cleaner Python API for batch requests (up to 120 symbols per call). For backtesting research where we're computing signals manually, it's marginal. 

Most interesting use case for us: **PEAD pipeline** — `get_earnings_calendar()` and `get_earnings()` could replace manual yfinance scraping for the earnings event detection in `pead_overnight.py`, with more reliable structured output.

Worth adding a free account and testing for that specific use case? Or is there a different...

<message id="1066" from="telegram-mg-17769" sender="Kevin Houston" time="May 15, 2026, 7:56 AM">Yes</message>

**George**: <message to="telegram-mg-17769">
Solid codebase. Here's what's inside:

**Architecture**

A 4-layer pipeline: Super Agent (triage) → Execution Planner (task decomposition via LLM) → Agent Orchestrator → Specialized Agents. The orchestrator runs async with background producers so a disconnected client doesn't kill a running plan. Clean separation throughout.

**The agents**

- **Research Agent** — Pulls SEC 8-K/10-K/10-Q via Edgar, web search (Gemini grounding or Perplexity), crypto data. Keeps 3 prior session summaries as context. Has a vector knowledge base (LanceDB) for semantic search over PDFs/markdown, gracefully degrades to tool-only mode if unavailable.
- **Strategy Agent** — Fetches candles + market snapshots concurrently, builds a JSON feature vector (technicals, portfolio metrics), passes to LLM which outputs a `TradePlanProposal` → validated `TradeInstruction`. Three risk profiles: Default (1-2% per trade), Aggressive (momentum/breakouts, dynamic ATR stops), and Insane.
- **Grid Agent** — Rule-based + LLM hybrid for crypto grid trading
- **News Agent** — Streaming, multi-source, language-matched

**Exchange layer**: CCXT giving access to 100+ exchanges. Auto-configures leverage mode, hedging mode, margin type per exchange. Paper trading via in-memory simulator. Alpaca isn't in there — it's pure crypto.

**Risk management is baked into prompts** — Sharpe ratio feedback loops that halt trading after 6+ cycles if Sharpe < -0.5, require 80%+ confidence if Sharpe is -0.5 to 0, relax constraints above 0.7. That's a pattern worth borrowing.

**Patterns directly relevant to us:**

1. **Regime gating via Sharpe feedback loop** — exactly the H165 circuit-breaker design. Their prompt-level Sharpe thresholds map cleanly to "exit to BIL if regime turns."

2. **Features pipeline + LLM composer split** — the `DefaultFeaturesPipeline` computes technicals, then `LlmComposer` makes the decision. Clean boundary. We could use the same split for PEAD: a deterministic pipeline...

<message id="1100" from="telegram-mg-17769" sender="Kevin Houston" time="May 15, 2026, 12:41 PM">Is this repo interesting https://github.com/supertone-inc/supertonic</message>

**George**: <message to="telegram-mg-17769">
Yes — **Supertonic** is genuinely interesting for us.

**What it is:** On-device, multilingual TTS from Supertone (Korean AI audio company). ONNX Runtime — no cloud, no API, no cost per call. `pip install supertonic`, runs locally.

**Why it's relevant:**
- **31 languages** (v3, released April 2026) — covers the Lithuanian phrase use case and anything multilingual going forward
- **Voice Builder** (live since Jan 2026) — you record your own voice, they generate a deployable edge-native TTS model. Permanent ownership. This is a path to a *George voice* that sounds like you or a character you define
- **Fast on CPU** — ONNX-optimized, designed for edge/local inference, competitive WER against much larger models
- **Multi-runtime** — Python, Node.js, browser (WASM), Go, Swift, C++, C#, Rust, iOS. Very portable

**Quick start:**
```python
from supertonic import TTS
tts = TTS(auto_download=True)  # downloads model from HuggingFace on first run
style = tts.get_voice_style(voice_name="M1")
wav, duration = tts.synthesize("Hello", style=style, lang="en")
tts.save_audio(wav, "output.wav")
```

**Versus current setup (gTTS):** gTTS is Google's cloud TTS, free but requires internet and has robotic quality. Supertonic is local, faster for repeated use, and higher quality.

Worth noting in the wiki. Want me to also try it on the Lithuanian phrase?
</message>
